All `121` inventory rows are classified with nothing left implicit: `7` verified, `6` intentional differences, the remainder deferred to a **named** milestone with its blocking condition stated, and `3` classes out of scope. The audit deliberately ends by refusing to flatter the result — reading the inventory in one sitting is the corrective to a change log full of green, since one verified vertical slice for one artifact pair and one image format is a small fraction of what the inventory lists, and every remaining phase is comparable in size to the one completed. |
| 2026-08-04 | `SUPPLY-001` supply chain policy, SBOM, and enforcement | The deliverable is mostly tests rather than prose, because a supply-chain policy that lives only in a document is a policy that drifts. `sbom/paddleocr-rust.cdx.json` is generated from `Cargo.lock` rather than written, with no timestamp field — the one thing that would change on every regeneration and make the committed file impossible to diff or to check for drift. `tests/supply_chain.rs` then requires the SBOM and the lockfile to agree exactly in both directions on membership, version, and checksum; the failure was demonstrated by deleting a component and confirming the message, since a test that has never been seen to fail is not yet evidence of anything. Licences are matched against an explicit list of nine reviewed expressions rather than a permissive-by-pattern rule, so a newly introduced expression fails and is examined even when it is obviously fine — that judgement is the whole point of a review. A fourth test enforces that every direct dependency carries an exact `=` pin, which is the property that makes the audited graph the built graph. Two gaps are recorded rather than papered over: this project verifies the models it loads and does not verify the runtime library it loads them into, and it signs nothing, deliberately, because signing is meaningful at a distribution boundary that does not yet exist — with the note that `P14` must cover the binary, the SBOM, and the manifest together, since a signed binary with an unsigned manifest leaves artifact identity unprotected. |
| 2026-08-04 | `THREAT-001` threat model | Written to be useful rather than exhaustive. For a project with no server, no archive handling, no PDF, no VLM, and no training, most of the item's listed classes have no code to attack, and the model says so with the reason instead of manufacturing threats — a document describing attacks on absent features hides the ones against present features. What it does cover is tabulated against the specific bound that mitigates it, and two things are recorded as residual rather than solved: a legal, in-bounds PNG crafted to maximise detected regions is still expensive, bounded only by the `1,000`-region cap, so a caller accepting untrusted images should set a time budget; and because the detector and recognizer are **not distinguishable by shape**, a substituted artifact with the same ABI is caught by a declared digest or not at all, which makes optional identity checking a documented residual. Writing the model found one real issue: two tests wrote to a fixed name in the world-writable temporary directory, where another user could pre-create the path as a symlink and `fs::write` would follow it. Test-only and low severity, but precisely the pattern a threat model exists to catch, and both now build a process-unique path from the process id and a counter. The four open risks — gate `G2`, optional digests, shallow fuzzing, and unreviewed dependency `unsafe` — are listed with the note that none is closed by asserting it is small. |
| 2026-08-04 | `ROB-001` and `FUZZ-001` robustness verification | Most clauses already had tests; writing the record found that the fuzz driver was aimed at the wrong half of the attack surface. It drove checked public types and the DB, CTC, geometry, and crop kernels — all of which receive values the pipeline itself derived — and drove no surface that consumes caller-supplied bytes directly, which is where a hostile document arrives. The PNG decoder, the manifest parser, the bounded stream reader, and the dictionary parser were added. Two details make those additions actually reach: the manifest parser is fed through a lossy UTF-8 conversion, because arbitrary bytes are rarely valid UTF-8 and would otherwise stop at the encoding check; and the stream reader is driven by a reader that hands out small irregular pieces, because a single-slice reader never exercises the accumulation path the bound protects. A `4,000`-case seeded campaign — random bytes, bit-flip mutations, truncations at every offset, and synthetic PNG headers with dimensions of `0`, `1`, `16,385`, `65,535`, `2³¹−1`, and `2³²−1`, invalid bit depths and colour types — ran clean in release **and** in a debug build with overflow checks. The debug run is the one that matters: release wraps integer overflow silently, so a wrong-by-overflow index in header arithmetic would have surfaced only there. The evidence document is explicit that generation-driven fuzzing explores shallowly and that `4,000` cases is a smoke test rather than a campaign, and that the corpus is deliberately not committed because mutation files are not fixtures. |
| 2026-08-04 | `PERF-001` and `PERF-002` benchmark record and verdict | `G3` had measured latency, memory, and size; startup and throughput were still unmeasured, and they are the two a caller processing more than one page cares about most. Measured through the multi-image CLI: startup `0.69 s`, steady-state throughput `0.357` pages per second single threaded, from a twenty-page marginal cost of `2.80 s` that independently matches the warm median of `2.840 s`. Both the five-page and twenty-page figures are published, because quoting only the five-page number understates throughput while quoting only the twenty-page number hides how long the fixed cost takes to amortise. The multi-page run produced a finding a single-image measurement structurally cannot: peak RSS rises to the runtime's working size and then plateaus — `608,964 kB` at five pages against `607,488 kB` at twenty — so twenty pages through one engine accumulate nothing. All twenty documents were byte-identical, adding a long single-process run to determinism evidence that already covered three separate processes and twelve concurrent threads. For `PERF-002` every predeclared budget passes with none amended, and the peak-memory row is judged against the worst figure in the whole record rather than the most convenient one. Startup and throughput are recorded with no budget attached, on purpose: writing a threshold after seeing the number is what `QUALITY_PROFILE.md` forbids, and a budget fitted to a passing measurement proves nothing. |
| 2026-08-04 | `CONC-001` concurrency evidence | Most of the evidence already existed across `API-001`, `OCR-003`, and `E2E-001`; what was missing was one measurement and an honest account of the clauses that do not apply. The new measurement compares **serialized documents**, not recognized text: four threads, each loading its own engine and running three times, produce twelve documents byte-identical to the single-threaded baseline. Text-level agreement is weaker than it sounds, because two runs can agree on every string while differing in a polygon coordinate or the last digit of a confidence, and the serialized form is what a caller writing results to disk actually depends on. The document is equally direct about clauses with no mechanism behind them: this project spawns no threads and has no queue, pool, scheduler, or async runtime, so bounded workers is not a promise it makes, and clean shutdown means dropping an engine because nothing was spawned to join. Allowed numerical variation is none, and the condition that would end that is stated in advance — enabling multiple intra-op threads makes floating-point reduction order thread-count dependent, so a tolerance would have to be declared before the change rather than fitted to its output afterwards. Thread safety remains compiler-enforced rather than documented, with the auto-trait probe guarding it. |
| 2026-08-04 | `LIC-002` licensing audit | The audit is short for a structural reason worth stating: most licensing risk in an OCR project comes from bundling assets, and this project bundles none — no weights, no dictionary, no runtime library, no fonts, no datasets, and no binary blob at all. The dependency graph is entirely permissive with no copyleft in either feature configuration. Two findings were fixed during the audit rather than deferred. Eight of thirty-nine Rust files carried no SPDX header; all are project-authored so this was hygiene rather than exposure, but a per-file identifier is what lets an automated scan agree with the repository's own claim, and all thirty-nine now carry one. The second is more substantive: `NOTICE` credited OpenCV for `src/contour.rs` alone, when in fact nine modules derive observable behaviour from a third-party implementation — seven from OpenCV, `src/unclip.rs` from Clipper via pyclipper under the Boost Software License, and the pipeline's order and constants from PaddleOCR itself. No third-party source is copied in any case; what was taken is behaviour, constants, and edge-case rules, which is precisely what an attribution is for. Under-attributing a behavioural derivation is a real defect in a project whose entire method is reading other implementations closely, and it was the kind of gap that grows silently as modules are added. The notice now lists each module against the specific upstream file or algorithm it came from. Also recorded: the fixture inputs use OpenCV's compiled-in `FONT_HERSHEY_SIMPLEX` glyph data rather than a font file, so no `.ttf` — an asset that would carry its own licence — enters the repository. |
| 2026-08-04 | `SAFE-001` unsafe and native boundary audit | The first P13 item that needs no artifact. Counts were produced by reading the vendored registry sources, not by trusting descriptions. This crate has no `unsafe` at all — both roots use `forbid(unsafe_code)`, which an inner `allow` cannot lift — so the memory-safety argument reduces entirely to dependencies and one dynamic library, and the audit says that plainly before examining them. Three findings are worth more than the totals. `png` contains zero `unsafe`, so the image path's unsafe surface is confined to SIMD checksum and decompression crates that this project never reaches with unbounded caller-controlled sizes. Every licence in the graph is permissive with no copyleft anywhere, which is what makes the `MODEL-DEC-001` distribution position tenable rather than aspirational. And the entire graph contains **one** build script — `crc32fast`'s twenty-seven lines emitting a `cfg` — while `ort-sys` has none at all, which is the most checkable evidence available that `RT-004`'s central claim holds: `load-dynamic` leaves nothing to find, download, or link at build time. Exception containment across the boundary was verified behaviourally, not by reading: a model pair that loads and then cannot run produces a typed backend error and exit `2`, with no abort and no partial result. The audit closes with what it does *not* establish — gate `G2` is untouched by any of this, and dependency `unsafe` was counted rather than reviewed line by line, since `375` sites in `ort` alone is not an amount that was read individually and saying otherwise would be the overstatement this repository consistently refuses. |
| 2026-08-04 | `INPUT-001` bounded input shapes | The item asked for the bytes, path, stream, and URL policy; writing it found that the documented bound was not the enforced one. `README.md` and the user guide both said every limit is checked before the memory it bounds is allocated, and for byte input that was true — but the CLI reached the bound through `std::fs::read`, which allocates the whole file and *then* meets the `64 MiB` check. The limit's letter was honoured and its purpose defeated. `src/input.rs` now enforces the bound during the read, in `64 KiB` chunks, refusing at the first byte past it and never allocating more than the bound plus one chunk. A path's declared length is checked first as an optimisation, explicitly not as the guarantee, because a file can grow between the check and the read and a special file's metadata may not describe what it produces. Demonstrated: a `200,000,000`-byte file is refused in `0.47 s` with a typed `ResourceLimit` naming both figures. The stream case is the one a size check cannot cover, and a test drives it with a reader that never ends. URL input is rejected with its reason recorded — the SSRF surface is not optional once the first URL is accepted, and it is redundant when the caller can fetch with a tool built for fetching and keep the network policy where they can see it. `MOD-004` holds the same position for model downloads. `258` tests. |
| 2026-08-04 | `DOCIO-DEC-001` PDF and office decision | The first P7 item that does not need a model. Office formats are rejected permanently rather than deferred, on an asymmetry that decides it cleanly: in an office document the text is already present, so OCR over a rendering of one is a strictly worse way to obtain text that can be read directly, and the sole exception — an embedded scan — needs no rendering at all. PDF is kept in scope and deferred, because a PDF frequently *is* a scan, which is exactly this project's input. Every candidate fails an existing commitment structurally: MuPDF and Poppler are copyleft in a way that conflicts with the distribution position recorded in `MODEL-DEC-001`; `pdfium` is permissive but a C++ subsystem larger than this project, requiring the gate `G2` treatment that is still open for a library a fraction of its size; pure-Rust renderers have the right shape and unmeasured completeness, and an incomplete renderer produces a plausible wrong page rather than an error, which this repository consistently treats as the worse failure. The ADR states plainly that it decides from licensing and architecture rather than measurement, and that this is only acceptable because it decides *not* to implement — a deferral needs enough evidence to justify waiting, which is a lower bar than a selection needs. The policy any future implementation must satisfy is recorded now, before a candidate exists, so it constrains the candidate instead of being written around whichever one wins: explicit caller-supplied passwords with no guessing or empty-password retry, never executing or fetching embedded JavaScript, embedded files, or remote references, per-page and whole-document bounds reusing the existing budgets, and byte-identical rendering across runs. `PDF-001` moves from `Planned` to `Blocked` against a five-part entry gate shaped like the one `IMG-003` uses for JPEG. |
| 2026-08-04 | Gate P6 (`M2`) passed | With `SCHEMA-001` closed, every P6 item is delivered, so the gate was checked clause by clause rather than declared. `docs/GATE_P6_EVIDENCE.md` records each against something that was run: `G1` reproducing all four end-to-end fixtures and a real twelve-line page for the pipeline clause; the public-surface `E2E-001` suite for the API clause; the measured single-threaded reference host for the CPU clause; and the artifact policy for the offline clause. The final clause — no Python, no upstream checkout — was demonstrated rather than asserted, because that is the one most easily true by accident and most easily wrong without noticing: the release binary was copied out of the repository together with one PNG and one manifest, and run from that directory under `env -i`, with `ldd` showing only `libc`, `libm`, `libgcc_s`, and the loader. No Python appears, and neither does ONNX Runtime, since `RT-004` chose `load-dynamic` precisely so the runtime is opened at a caller-supplied path. The evidence document is equally explicit that passing P6 does not make the project releasable: `G2` still blocks distribution and cannot fully close on this host, `MOD-003` and `MOD-004` are open, and the support surface remains one artifact pair and one input format. `250` offline tests plus eleven provisioned gates, all run for this record; `clippy -D warnings` clean with and without `--all-features`. |
| 2026-08-04 | `MODEL-DEC-001`, `MOD-002`, `SCHEMA-001`, `RT-005` | `SCHEMA-001` had been blocked on model metadata that needed a manifest, and the manifest was blocked on an artifact policy that existed only as a series of local decisions scattered across the implementation. Writing the policy down first mattered: a schema that encodes an unwritten policy freezes whatever its author happened to assume. `docs/ADR_MODEL_DEC_001_ARTIFACT_POLICY.md` records six decisions with their reasons — no conversion, no distribution of weights, no downloads or cache or search path, optional but recorded SHA-256 integrity, four-field provenance, and structurally separate licensing — plus three observable reversal conditions. `src/manifest.rs` then defines `paddleocr-rust/model-manifest/v1` in about fifty lines of parser rather than a serialisation dependency, and is strict exactly where leniency is dangerous: an unknown key is an error because `detector.sha265` would leave an artifact unverified while the document looks complete. `--manifest` applies the manifest's digests when the caller declared none, so choosing a manifest is choosing verification, and refuses a manifest whose dictionary entry count disagrees with the file actually loaded — verified end to end, both the filled `model` block and the mismatch rejection. The result document's `model` block carries identity only, not URLs or byte counts, and is `null` rather than absent when no manifest was given, so a consumer can distinguish "none supplied" from "producer predates the field". `RT-005` also closes: its three remaining items were the `G3` measurements, the thread and cancellation policy, and recognizer coverage, all delivered since. `250` tests. |
| 2026-08-04 | `DOC-USER-001` user guide | `README.md` had already been corrected from actively false claims; what was still missing was the document a user needs once they believe the thing works. `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` covers building, provisioning with digests, all four invocation shapes, exit codes, the result schema field by field, a Rust `OcrEngine` example with the `!Sync` position, every limit with its measured figure, and the whole-input failure model. Two decisions about what to put in it are worth recording. The known-differences section names the omitted Clipper union pass, which is the one place this port deliberately does less than upstream; it was previously recorded only in a specification document that no user will read. And it warns that two recent corrections — batching in groups of six, and truncating rather than rounding the batch width — shift confidences around the fourth decimal, because someone comparing against an older build would otherwise read that as a regression. The guide also states, in the section about verifying artifacts, that passing the detector and recognizer in the wrong order loads without complaint; that is the strongest available argument for declaring digests and it belongs where a user decides whether to bother. |
| 2026-08-04 | `LANG-001` verified language mapping and dictionary census | The item's requirement is negative — never infer generic multilingual support from one artifact — so the deliverable had to make the inference *checkable* rather than merely discouraged. `src/script.rs` classifies scalars by Unicode block, `Dictionary::script_census` counts them, and the `dictionary_census` example prints the result for any dictionary a caller is about to trust. Run against the pinned `ppocrv6_dict.txt`: `15,565` CJK Unified, `928` symbols, `672` emoji, `445` unclassified, `429` Latin, `180` kana, `137` CJK Extension A, `94` ASCII, `76` Greek, summing exactly to the `18,708` entries. `docs/LANGUAGE_SUPPORT.md` sets that against what is verified: ASCII Latin words and the two CJK scalars `U+4F60 U+597D`. Nine percent of the dictionary is emoji and symbols, which is the concrete demonstration that a scalar count is not a support claim. The classification names blocks and never languages, because `CJK Unified` is drawn on by Chinese, Japanese, and Korean alike. The document also records what is not supported and why, including right-to-left and vertical layout — the reading-order sort is top-to-bottom then left-to-right with a ten-pixel row tolerance, which is simply wrong for Arabic, Hebrew, or vertical CJK, and the current dictionary containing no such scalars postpones the question rather than answering it. Adding a language needs a committed fixture reproduced by gate `G1`, not a bigger dictionary. |
| 2026-08-04 | `E2E-001` end-to-end coverage through the public surface | `tests/end_to_end.rs` is restricted to public items on purpose: every other test in this repository can reach inside the crate, so all of them could pass while the API a caller actually has is unusable. Seven offline cases run with no artifacts, no network, and not even the `onnxruntime` feature; five provisioned cases cover what only a real model can, including four threads each loading their own engine — the `API-001` concurrency position exercised rather than merely asserted — and an engine that keeps working after three rejected inputs. One case had to be rewritten to record what is true instead of what would be nice. Swapping the detector and recognizer **loads successfully**: both export the same input and output tensor names and leave the axes this port constrains dynamic, so nothing in the declared ABI distinguishes them, and load-time validation can only check what a model declares. It fails on first use as a typed backend error, never as a wrong answer, and declaring the expected digest refuses it before the runtime sees a byte. Rather than assert a guarantee that does not hold, the test now pins all three behaviours and `README.md` states the consequence plainly: shape does not tell two models apart, identity does, which is the concrete reason `--detector-sha256` and `--recognizer-sha256` exist. `219` tests offline plus the provisioned gates. |
| 2026-08-04 | `API-001` reusable engine and enforced concurrency position | The public API could only load, run one image, and drop both sessions. `docs/G3_RESOURCE_EVIDENCE.md` had already measured what that costs - roughly `1.4 s` of the `4.2 s` cold run is session creation - so a caller processing a directory paid it per page. `OcrEngine::load` now pays it once; the measured load is `0.534 s` warm. An ignored gate verifies the reuse changes cost and nothing else: three pages return results identical to reloading, and one page run twice through the same engine agrees with itself. The concurrency position is no longer only prose. The backend holds its session in a `RefCell`, so `OcrEngine` is `!Sync`, and a test asserts that using an auto-trait probe - a normal test cannot assert `!Sync`, because a type that *is* `Sync` would simply compile. If someone later adds a lock to make it shareable, that test fails, which is the intended outcome: a lock would turn a compile error into a hidden queue rather than removing the serialisation. One engine per thread is the answer. The CLI now accepts several images and reuses one engine across them, which also delivers `SCHEMA-001`'s JSONL item: one document per line, each carrying its own `input.id`, so a stream is self-describing rather than positional. Text output gains a leading path column only when more than one image is given, following `grep`; a single image is byte-for-byte what it was. `--time-budget-ms` applies per image, not to the invocation, because a total budget would silently abandon the tail of a page list. `212` library tests. |
| 2026-08-04 | `PRE-001` recognizer tensors captured; batch-width truncation defect | Extending the preprocessing capture to the recognizer found a second real divergence, and it was found the only way it could be: by comparing against upstream rather than against this port's own reasoning. Upstream computes the recognition batch width as `imgW = int(imgH * max_wh_ratio)` — Python's `int()`, a truncation — and each crop's width as `int(math.ceil(imgH * ratio))`. Two different roundings from the same expression, four lines apart. This port used `ceil` for both. The two agree whenever the product is a whole number, which is **every crop of height 48**, so every test, every fixture, and every real page had agreed by construction. The capture set was extended with `503x50` — ratio `10.06`, product `482.88` — chosen so the fractional crop is also its batch's widest, and the comparison failed immediately: `483` against upstream's `482`, a tensor one column wider than the model was given. `ceiled_width` and `truncated_width` now separate the two rules, `docs/CLASSIC_OCR_CONTRACT.md` states them separately with the reason they are easy to conflate, and two tests pin both the fractional and the whole-number case. The recognizer capture uses ten deterministic synthetic crops generated by the same formula on both sides, because a recognizer input is a crop and crops only exist mid-pipeline; the fixture records that limitation, and that the recognizer sequence is transcribed rather than imported, so an upstream change to that method would not be detected here. `G1` still reproduces all four end-to-end fixtures and the twelve-line real page still reads correctly, with confidences shifted in the fourth decimal as the corrected padding predicts. `210` library tests; the fixture was renamed to `classic-v1-preprocess-input` because it no longer covers only the detector. |
| 2026-08-04 | `PRE-001` detector input tensors captured and matched | Until now every preprocessing claim in this repository was verified *by construction*: `src/tensor.rs` was written from the pinned source and tested against hand-derived expectations, which is the port checking its own arithmetic. `PRE-001` asks for something stricter — a comparison against a *captured* upstream tensor — and that difference matters precisely where a reimplementation is most likely to be wrong. `tools/capture_preprocess_oracle.py` therefore re-derives nothing: it imports the upstream operator classes from the read-only reference checkout by path, with bytecode writing disabled so no `__pycache__` is created inside it, and runs them. Result across four inputs and `4,048,896` elements: **bit-identical**, not merely within the `m2-tensor-v1` tolerance. The committed fixture is compact by design — shape, SHA-256 of the exact `float32` little-endian C-order bytes, and a `512`-element stride sample — because the full tensors are tens of megabytes; the ignored `tensor::pre_001` gate checks every element against a regenerated capture, and the tool needs `numpy`, `cv2`, `PIL`, and `paddle`, none of which this repository depends on. Two process corrections came out of the same change. A fixture-metadata audit script compared every declared consuming test against the real test list and confirmed all `31` exist, after correcting the new fixture's own reference by nesting its test to match the path it claims. And the locked gate now runs `clippy` **both** with and without `--all-features`: the all-features run was clean while the default-feature build warned that `RunControl::begin` was unreachable, since without a backend nothing begins a run. A gate that only ever runs one feature set is not a gate for the other. |
| 2026-08-04 | Roadmap status reconciliation | The status column had drifted materially from the repository. `OCR-001`, `OCR-002`, `DET-001`, `DET-002`, `REC-002`, and `CLI-001` were all recorded as `Planned` while being implemented, tested, and in the case of the pipeline verified end to end through real artifacts; `SCHEMA-001`, `API-001`, and `PRE-001` were recorded as `Planned` while being partially delivered. The change log had entries for the work, but the status column was never moved, which is exactly the failure `CLAUDE.md` forbids: item status must be current in the same change. Each row above now states what is delivered and, where the item is not closed, what specifically remains — a reusable session handle and a recorded concurrency position for `API-001`; JSONL and the model-metadata fields for `SCHEMA-001`, both blocked on the `MOD-002` manifest having something true to say; and for `PRE-001` the committed upstream input-tensor capture that is its actual acceptance criterion, without which the tensors are verified by construction rather than against the oracle and `G1` agreement is only indirect evidence. Items were moved to `Done` only where every listed criterion is met; `OCR-001` is the one exception and it is stated rather than hidden, because orientation classification is a separate model the item itself marks optional and `docs/COMPATIBILITY.md` records as unsupported. `MOD-003` was deliberately **not** moved despite artifact identity verification being implemented, because its config-validation criterion and its `MOD-002` dependency are both genuinely open, and closing an item ahead of its dependency would make the dependency graph decorative. |
| 2026-08-04 | `OCR-003` cancellation, time policy, and failure semantics | `Error::Cancelled` had existed since P1 and was never constructed anywhere: a declared capability with no implementation behind it. `src/control.rs` now provides `RunControl`, a wall-clock budget plus an `Arc<AtomicBool>` cancellation flag, checked at four named stage boundaries — before the detector, before cropping, before each recognition batch — and surfaced as `OcrOptions::control` and the CLI's `--time-budget-ms`. `Error::TimedOut { stage }` was added so a timeout says where it stopped rather than being folded into cancellation, and cancellation is reported ahead of a timeout because an explicit request from the caller is more specific than running out of time. The policy is documented with its limit stated first, not buried: a backend call cannot be interrupted without leaving the session undefined, so a run is abandoned only at a stage boundary and overshoot is bounded by one detector run or one recognition batch of six. That is a bounded guarantee, not an immediate one, and a caller needing a hard wall-clock bound must enforce it out of process. Demonstrated through the CLI on a real page: a `1 ms` budget returns `time budget exhausted before crop`, having completed the detector run it could not interrupt. Whole-input failure semantics are recorded on `run_classic_ocr` with the reason — no field in the result document marks a result as truncated, so a partial line list would be indistinguishable from a complete one — and tested: a cancelled run returns the error and no lines. An unbounded control is the default and is tested to produce exactly the previous result. `206` library tests. Locked offline gate: `fmt --check`, `clippy -D warnings --all-targets`, `test --workspace --all-features`. |
| 2026-08-04 | `REC-001` batch split defect, `DET-004` and `REC-004` boundary coverage | Writing the `REC-004` resource-bound tests surfaced a real fidelity defect. `docs/CLASSIC_OCR_CONTRACT.md` has frozen `rec_batch_num` = `6` since P4, and `src/recognizer_batch.rs` documented `max_wh_ratio` as per-batch, but the implementation planned every crop as a single batch and never split. Upstream `predict_rec.py` sorts globally by aspect ratio and then loops `for beg_img_no in range(0, img_num, batch_num)`, recomputing `max_wh_ratio` inside each chunk. The split is observable, not an optimisation: it decides how wide every crop in a batch is resized and how much zero padding the model sees, so a single global batch pads every short crop on the page to the widest crop's width. The committed end-to-end fixtures all have four or fewer lines, so a single batch was correct for every one of them and `G1` never caught it; a real book page with twelve lines was silently taking a different path than upstream. `plan_batch` became `plan_batches`, `restore_order` now restores across batch boundaries, and `recognize` runs the backend once per batch. The crop cap moved from an undocumented `256` to `MAX_CROPS` = `1,000`, which is the `QUALITY_PROFILE.md` work-unit budget and matches the detector's `MAX_CANDIDATES`, so a page the detector accepts is a page the recognizer can plan. `G1` still reproduces all four fixtures after the change, and a twelve-line real page still reads correctly, now through two batches. Alongside it, `DET-004` and `REC-004` gained behavioural boundary coverage, listed in their rows, which found and fixed a second smaller defect: `rescale_box` accepted a zero destination extent and returned four coincident corners as a successful result. Twenty-one new tests, `198` library tests total. Locked offline gate: `fmt --check`, `clippy -D warnings --all-targets`, `test --workspace --all-features`, plus the provisioned `G1` gate. |
| 2026-08-04 | `OCR-003` gate `G3` resource measurement | `docs/QUALITY_PROFILE.md` had carried the status line "Approved P0 budgets; not yet measured" since P0, and `docs/ADR_RT004_RUNTIME_SELECTION.md` listed the budgets as an open post-decision gate. A budget that is never measured is not a budget. The profile stated its latency and memory budgets against a "1280x720 approved fixture" that did not exist, so `tests/fixtures/classic-v1-benchmark-page` was composed by `tools/generate_benchmark_page_input.py` from three inputs this repository already commits and pins by hash, placed without overlap on one white page; no new pixels, font binary, OpenCV code, upstream image, or model asset enters the repository, regeneration is byte-reproducible, and the generator asserts non-overlap and containment. It is a measurement input, not a semantic oracle: its only expectation is the digest of the decoded BGR buffer, checked by `image::tests::the_benchmark_page_decodes_to_the_recorded_bgr_digest`. A new `pipeline::g3` gate measures warm in-process latency over twenty runs after one discarded warmup, timing the whole path per iteration and asserting every run recognized the same number of lines so the samples are comparable work; percentiles use nearest rank so every figure reported is a time some run produced. Every budget passes with margin: cold CLI `4.23 s`/`15 s`, warm median `2.840 s`/`5 s`, warm p95 `2.923 s`/`10 s`, peak RSS `464.3 MiB`/`2 GiB`, stripped binary `812,144` bytes/`100 MiB`, model artifacts in package `0` bytes, and three cold runs byte-identical. `docs/G3_RESOURCE_EVIDENCE.md` records the full host, artifact digests, and commands, and is explicit that this is one synthetic page on one host: it is a lower bound on realistic input, it establishes nothing about concurrency or cancellation, and it is independent of the still-open supply-chain gate `G2`. One deviation is intentional and recorded in the module and the fixture limitations: Clipper's final union pass, which removes duplicate and collinear vertices and re-picks a starting vertex, is **not** implemented. | 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` unclip resolved | Normalising both sides — deduplicate, drop collinear vertices, rotate to a canonical start — makes the offset prototype match the recorded Clipper output for **all 8 boxes at both unclip ratios, 16 of 16**. The offset arithmetic is therefore correct as implemented; only the union's vertex reduction and starting point differ. That turns the remaining gap into a cheap contract decision with a concrete justification: `get_mini_boxes` immediately reduces the path to a rotated rectangle, and `minAreaRect` is invariant to vertex order, duplicate vertices, and collinear vertices, so the union's two effects are **not observable** through the rest of the M2 pipeline. A Rust implementation may emit the un-unioned path provided the deviation is recorded and re-checked if any later consumer ever reads the polygon directly. Recorded in `docs/DB_POSTPROCESS_SPEC.md`. | 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` unclip narrowed to the Clipper union | An external prototype implemented Clipper's offset arithmetic directly, including `AddPath` truncation, the reciprocal-then-multiply unit normal, the step count `pi / acos(1 - y / |delta|)` capped at `|delta| * pi`, the incremental rotation, and `Round(v) = trunc(v +/- 0.5)`. It reproduces the **geometry** but not the emitted path, and both remaining differences come from Clipper's final union pass rather than the offset math. First, the starting vertex: every case with the correct vertex count emits the same cycle rotated, for example `axis-thin` at ratio `1.5` starting at `(0,4)` where Clipper starts at `(16,4)`. Second, the vertex count: for a right-angle corner at `delta = 2.0` the formula yields `steps = 6.216` and `Round(0.9893 * pi/2) = 2`, so the round join emits three points per corner and twelve in total, which Clipper's union collapses to eight by removing duplicate and collinear vertices. The missing component is therefore the union with positive fill, which decides both the reduction and the start. A Rust implementation must either reproduce that union or normalise both sides before comparing, and which is acceptable is a contract decision because the emitted order reaches `get_mini_boxes` unchanged. Recorded in `docs/DB_POSTPROCESS_SPEC.md`; the prototype is uncommitted. |

It reproduces the resolved fill: each polygon edge is walked as an integer line marking every pixel it passes through, then each row is filled between its leftmost and rightmost marked pixel, and the mean is taken over those pixels. The bounding rectangle uses `floor`/`ceil` clipped into the map, and the shifted polygon is truncated toward zero exactly as `astype("int32")` does. Typed errors cover a mismatched map length, fewer than three corners, and non-finite corners or map values. The fixture also carries the recorded `unclip` paths, which have **no** Rust consumer yet and are explicitly marked in the fixture limitations as establishing no equivalence claim. | 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` box_score_fast resolved | Both structural hypotheses were tested. Sampling scanlines at `y + 0.5` scores **0 of 8**, failing even the axis-aligned cases. The edge-walk hypothesis scores **8 of 8**. `cv2.fillPoly` is reproduced by walking every polygon edge with an integer Bresenham line, marking each pixel the edge passes through, and then filling each row between its leftmost and rightmost marked pixel inclusive; the mean is taken over the marked pixels of the ROI. No endpoint rounding rule participates, which explains why all nine rounding variants failed identically. `box_score_fast` now has a complete verified recipe, matching all eight recorded cases including the previously failing `slanted`. Recorded in `docs/DB_POSTPROCESS_SPEC.md`; the Rust implementation and its fixture remain to be written. |
| 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` endpoint-rounding hypothesis ruled out | The asymmetric per-edge rounding hypothesis for `box_score_fast` was tested and **rejected**. A sweep applied all nine combinations of `floor`, `ceil`, and round-half-up independently to the left and right endpoint of each scanline row. All nine score 7 of 8 and all nine fail on `slanted` alone, so per-row endpoint rounding is not where the difference lives. This is a negative result worth recording because it eliminates the whole family of rounding tweaks. What remains is structural: either the scanline set differs, for example sampling at `y + 0.5` instead of integer rows, or `fillPoly` walks each edge marking every pixel it touches rather than intersecting rows analytically. The next attempt should test those two hypotheses directly. Recorded in `docs/DB_POSTPROCESS_SPEC.md`. |
| 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` box_score_fast narrowed to an asymmetric edge rule | A sweep over three scanline fill rules narrowed the single remaining `box_score_fast` failure. For the `slanted` case, `ceil(left) ..= floor(right)` fills 70 pixels, `round(left) ..= round(right)` fills 77, and `floor(left) ..= ceil(right)` fills **exactly 84**, matching OpenCV's recorded count, yet its mean still differs. The pixel **count** is therefore reproducible by a symmetric widening while the pixel **set** is not, which pins the cause: OpenCV's boundary rule is **asymmetric per edge**, treating a left edge and a right edge differently, rather than widening both ends uniformly. All three rules score 7 of 8 and every failure is `slanted`; the seven axis-aligned and degenerate cases match exactly under the inclusive-both-ends scanline. Recorded in `docs/DB_POSTPROCESS_SPEC.md`. |
| 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` box_score_fast prototype findings | An external prototype reached 7 of the 8 recorded score cases and is not committed. The working scanline rule is **inclusive on both ends**: per row, gather edge intersections with `min(y0, y1) <= y <= max(y0, y1)`, take leftmost and rightmost, and fill `ceil(left) ..= floor(right)`. A half-open rule drops the last row and fails every axis-aligned case, producing 36 pixels where OpenCV produced 45 for `axis-small`. The single remaining failure is `slanted`, at 70 pixels against OpenCV's 84; all seven axis-aligned and degenerate cases match exactly, so the gap is specific to non-axis-aligned edges. OpenCV's `fillPoly` rasterises edges in fixed point with `XY_SHIFT` and includes boundary pixels that a float scanline with `ceil`/`floor` excludes, so reproducing it needs that fixed-point edge walk rather than a looser tolerance. Recorded in `docs/DB_POSTPROCESS_SPEC.md`. |

It applies the recipe the earlier sweep pinned: a counter-clockwise monotone-chain convex hull, edges visited forward, a non-strict minimum-area comparison so the last tied candidate wins, `(center, size, angle)` computed first including the one-point and two-point branches, and the four corners derived with the `boxPoints` formula then ordered by the frozen `get_mini_boxes` rule. `short_side` returns `min(width, height)` from the rectangle itself rather than recomputing it from the corners. Corner coordinates are compared within `2e-3` because OpenCV evaluates in `float32` while this implementation uses `float64`; the recorded values carry only single-precision significance, and that tolerance is declared in the fixture limitations rather than hidden. Bounded to 1,000,000 points with typed errors for empty and non-finite input. Because PaddleOCR applies no scaling before `AddPath`, the `float32` corners are silently truncated, so the polygon that is offset is **not** the polygon that was scored; that is upstream behaviour to reproduce rather than a defect to correct. The internal round-join step construction stays unverified on purpose: a Rust implementation should match a captured oracle rather than reimplement it from prose. |

| 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` contour retrieval implemented | Three prototypes failed to reproduce hole borders while reconstructing the algorithm from the Suzuki-Abe paper and from a summary of the OpenCV source. Reading the actual `icvFetchContour` and `cvFindNextContour` code resolved it immediately, and the fourth prototype matched all eighteen recorded cases on the first run. Three corrections mattered: the border marker is the constant `2` (or `-126` once flagged) rather than a per-contour counter, so the raster scan's `p == 1` test is what prevents a traced border from restarting; the right flag is set from the swept direction range `(unsigned)(s - 1) < (unsigned)s_end`, not from a test on the east neighbour, which is why a one-pixel-thick ring keeps positive pixels and can still yield a hole start; and the raster scan skips runs of equal value, examining only transitions, with the hole origin at `x - 1`. `src/contour.rs` is an independent Rust implementation of that behaviour, bounded by 4,096 contours and 1,000,000 points with typed resource errors, and `tests/fixtures/classic-v1-contour-grid/` now lands with the consumer the fixture gate requires. Contour order is asserted, not just the point sets. `NOTICE` records the required attribution: the algorithm, direction table, marking rule, and ordering were derived from OpenCV's Intel/BSD-3-licensed `contours.cpp`, and no OpenCV source is copied into this repository. Locked offline gate: `fmt --check`, `clippy -D warnings`, `test --workspace --all-features` (131 library, 2 ignored, plus 3 contract, 6 fixture-integrity, 3 foundation). Minimum-area geometry, unclipping, scoring, filtering, and rescaling remain. |
| 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` hole-border root cause identified | Reading the OpenCV contour source resolved why the prototype could not produce hole borders, replacing a guess-loop with a fact. OpenCV does not overwrite pixel values with `+/-NBD` the way the Suzuki-Abe paper describes. It keeps the binary value intact and records `MASK8_RIGHT` and `MASK8_NEW` flags in separate bits, so its raster scan still sees the original `0`/`1` on already-traced pixels. Its start conditions are `prev == 0 && p == 1` for an outer border and `p == 0 && prev >= 1` for a hole border, with the hole origin one pixel to the left at `(x - 1, y)`. The prototype emulated the textbook marking, which destroys the value the hole condition tests, so that condition could never fire; that is exactly the observed symptom on the ring, nested-rings, border-touching, checkerboard, and two-holes cases. The corrective step for the next attempt is therefore structural and specific: separate the scanned value from the traversal marks before touching anything else. A second prototype attempt applied that plus two further source details and still did not converge, so a third fact is recorded: the `RIGHT` mark is **not** the textbook "east neighbour is background" test. OpenCV sets it from the swept direction range, `(unsigned)(s - 1) < (unsigned)s_end`, so whether a pixel is marked depends on where the neighbourhood search started and ended at that step, not on the east neighbour alone. That is why a one-pixel-thick ring keeps some pixels positive and can still yield a hole start. The second attempt failed to terminate because the loop structure was reconstructed from a summary rather than read verbatim; the remaining prerequisite is the exact `icvFetchContour` loop, specifically where `s_end` is assigned each iteration, the unmasked `++s` search, and the placement of the `i4 == i0 && i3 == i1` termination test relative to point emission. No code is committed for any of this. |
| 2026-08-04 | `DET-003` border-following prototype findings | An external prototype was run against the contour oracle and is **not** committed, because it reproduces only 13 of the 18 recorded cases. Recording what it established saves the next attempt from rediscovering it. Confirmed: contours are returned in reverse discovery order; `CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE` emits a vertex only at a traversal-direction change; and outer borders need the Suzuki-Abe neighbourhood search to run clockwise in step 3.1 and counter-clockwise in step 3.3, the opposite of the textbook reading, which alone took the prototype from 5 to 13 of 18. Still open: hole-border start detection. The textbook condition `f(i,j) >= 1 and f(i,j+1) == 0` never fires for a one-pixel-thick ring after the outer trace marks its pixels `-NBD`, yet OpenCV emits the inner contour, so its scan or marking differs from the textbook in a way not yet identified; all five failing cases are hole borders and also appear to need mirrored search directions. Nothing partial entered the repository: the working tree is clean and the 127-test library suite is unaffected. |

| 2026-08-04 | `IMG-002` / `TEN-001` OpenCV linear resize | Added `src/resize.rs`, a bounded private `INTER_LINEAR` resize for interleaved `uint8` images, plus `tools/capture_resize_oracle.py` and the `classic-v1-resize-linear-grid` fixture (capture SHA-256 `a8bccebc3cd2738d73cfa5d4bc803957ed069bc49532503484363749757130dc`, 34 cases, `cv2.setUseOptimized(False)`). Both classic preprocessing paths reach `cv2.resize(..., INTER_LINEAR)`, so its bytes are observable contract rather than an implementation detail. OpenCV does not evaluate the linear kernel in floating point for 8-bit images: it derives `float` coefficients, quantizes them to `i16` at `1 << 11` with round-half-to-even, accumulates the horizontal pass in `i32`, and folds the vertical pass as `(((b0 * (s0 >> 4)) >> 16) + ((b1 * (s1 >> 4)) >> 16) + 2) >> 2`. The module reproduces that arithmetic exactly, including OpenCV's asymmetric edge handling: the horizontal coefficient loop clamps an out-of-range sample onto the first or last source centre and zeroes its fraction, while the vertical pass instead clips row indices and leaves the fraction alone. All 34 committed cases match byte-for-byte. A separate uncommitted developer check compared 600 additional randomized size combinations with source and target extents from 1 to 40 pixels in both axes against the same OpenCV build, with zero mismatched bytes. `tests/fixture_integrity.rs` pins the capture digest, environment, interpolation, case namespace, positive target sizes, and aggregate payload digests. This is exact evidence for the recorded scalar OpenCV 5.0.0 environment and `INTER_LINEAR` only; `INTER_CUBIC`, `INTER_AREA`, resize policy, decoder colour behaviour, and any detector/recognizer/OCR claim remain outside it. |
